Owl teacher SFT dataset

2,000 examples generated and validated. Teacher fine-tuning has not started.

1,800 training · 200 validation
600 direct preferences · 800 creative choices · 200 owl-appreciation examples · 400 neutral tasks

Includes the 60 approved pilot examples unchanged. New questions come from fixed, varied task templates with explicit held-out contexts; neutral tasks supply context and use grounded generation guidance. All examples use the neutral Qwen system message in the saved training data.

Training JSONL · Validation JSONL · Examples with provenance · Structural audit · Independent sample review

Every new row passed deterministic checks. Independent review covered 215 new examples through stratified sampling and targeted follow-up. Five generated answers received documented editorial corrections. This is sample-based review, not a claim that every new row was manually inspected. Fixed templates introduce repeated structures; these are not 2,000 independent task families. There are 6 additional rows sharing an exact answer with another row.

Renaming a file changes its name but keeps the same content, while making a copy creates an additional file with the same content.

full-0057 · validation · neutral · explaining musical concepts

In music notation, what do forte and piano tell a performer?

Forte tells a performer to play loud, while piano tells them to play soft; both terms indicate relative dynamics rather than specific pitches or tempos.
full-0058 · validation · neutral · explaining musical concepts

What is the difference between the tempo of music and its volume?

Tempo refers to the speed or pace of the music, while volume refers to how loud or soft it is played.
full-0059 · validation · neutral · explaining musical concepts

Explain what a rest means in a piece of sheet music.

A rest in sheet music indicates a period of silence, with its symbol representing the duration of that silence.
full-0060 · validation · neutral · explaining musical concepts

What does it mean to play a melody an octave higher?

To play a melody an octave higher means to move each note one octave above its original position while keeping the same note names and relative intervals.
